Image name: PG_task2_gt2_PAIR5
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 6
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
1.  **Identify Target Nodes:** The two black nodes are M and B. My goal is to disconnect M from B by removing the minimum number of white nodes.

2.  **Analyze M's Connections:** Node M has two direct neighbors: J and L. This means any path from M to B must start by going through either J or L.

3.  **Analyze B's Connections:** Node B has three direct neighbors: N, C, and E. Any path from M to B must end by reaching B through one of these nodes.

4.  **Evaluate Single Node Removal:**
    *   If I remove J, paths like M-L-E-B still exist.
    *   If I remove L, paths like M-J-D-P-O-N-B still exist.
    *   If I remove E, paths like M-J-D-P-O-N-B still exist.
    *   If I remove N, paths like M-J-D-P-O-C-B and M-L-E-B still exist.
    *   If I remove C, paths like M-J-D-P-O-N-B and M-L-E-B still exist.
    *   If I remove any other single node (D, P, O, Y, I, H, X, K, F, G), there will always be at least one path remaining between M and B. For example, removing D leaves M-J-I-H-C-B and M-L-E-B.
    *   Therefore, a single node removal is not sufficient to disconnect M and B. The minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 2.

5.  **Identify Potential Cut Sets of Size 2:**
    *   **Option 1: Removing M's direct neighbors.** If I remove both J and L, M becomes isolated from the rest of the graph, and thus from B.
        *   Removing J severs all paths starting M -> J -> ... -> B.
        *   Removing L severs all paths starting M -> L -> ... -> B.
        *   Therefore, the set [J, L] successfully disconnects M from B. This is a cut set of size 2.

    *   **Option 2: Considering other critical nodes.**
        *   Let's consider the paths from M that go through L: M -> L -> E -> B. There are also longer paths like M -> L -> E -> F -> K -> ... -> B. All paths from M through L must pass through E. So, removing E would cut off all paths from M via L.
        *   If E is removed, we still need to cut off all paths from M that go through J. These paths include:
            *   M-J-D-P-O-N-B
            *   M-J-D-P-O-Y-N-B
            *   M-J-D-P-O-C-B
            *   M-J-I-H-C-B
            *   (Paths like M-J-I-H-X-K-F-E-B are already broken by removing E).
        *   All these remaining paths start with M -> J. Therefore, removing J would sever all of them.
        *   So, the set [E, J] also successfully disconnects M from B. This is another cut set of size 2.

6.  **Conclusion:** Since a single node removal is not sufficient, and I have found multiple cut sets of size 2, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 2.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [J, L]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 2, 'cut_set': ['J', 'E']}

Image name: FD-K_task3_gt2_PAIR5
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 6
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The target nodes are L and J. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges that must be removed to ensure no path exists between L and J.

1.  **Identify the degree of the target nodes:**
    *   Node L is connected to E and M. Its degree is 2.
    *   Node J is connected to M, I, P, and D. Its degree is 4.

2.  **Consider the minimum number of edges required:**
    *   If we remove only one edge connected to L, say (L, E), then L is still connected to M via (L, M). Since M is connected to J via (M, J), a path L-M-J still exists.
    *   If we remove only one edge connected to L, say (L, M), then L is still connected to E via (L, E). From E, there are multiple paths to J (e.g., L-E-H-I-J, L-E-M-J). Thus, a path still exists.
    *   Since removing a single edge does not disconnect L from J, the minimum number of edges to remove must be at least 2.

3.  **Find a cut set of size 2:**
    *   **Option 1: Isolate node L.**
        By removing both edges connected to L, namely (L, E) and (L, M), node L becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph. This ensures that no path can exist between L and J.
        Cut Set: [(L, E), (L, M)]

    *   **Option 2: Cut specific paths.**
        Let's consider the paths from L to J. All paths must either start with (L, E) or (L, M).
        *   Paths starting with L-E: L-E-M-J, L-E-H-I-J, L-E-H-B-C-I-J, L-E-H-B-C-O-P-J, L-E-H-B-N-O-P-J, and all paths through F-G-H.
        *   Paths starting with L-M: L-M-J.

        If we remove the edge (L, E), all paths starting L-E-... are broken. The only remaining path from L to J would be L-M-J. To break this path, we can remove the edge (M, J).
        Cut Set: [(L, E), (M, J)]
        Let's verify this cut set:
        *   Removing (L, E) breaks all paths that originate from L and go through E.
        *   After (L, E) is removed, the only way L can reach J is through M. The path is L-M-J.
        *   Removing (M, J) breaks this last remaining path.
        Therefore, L and J are disconnected.

4.  **Conclusion:** Both options provide a cut set of size 2. Since we established that at least 2 edges are required, 2 is the minimum number of edges.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(L, E), (L, M)]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 2, 'cut_set': [['E', 'L'], ['L', 'M']]}

Image name: POGBa_task2_gt2_PAIR5
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 6
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
1.  **Identify Target Nodes:** The two black nodes are M and B.
2.  **Analyze Connectivity:** I need to find all paths between M and B and identify critical nodes whose removal would disconnect them. I cannot remove M or B.
3.  **Identify Direct Neighbors:**
    *   Neighbors of M: L, E, K
    *   Neighbors of B: E, H, C, N
4.  **Initial Path Exploration and Bottlenecks:**
    *   Notice that node E is a common neighbor to both M and B (M-E-B is a direct path).
    *   Other paths from M must go through L or K.
    *   Other paths to B must come through H, C, or N.

5.  **Consider Single Node Removal:**
    *   If I remove L: M can still reach B via E (e.g., M-E-B) or K (e.g., M-K-X-G-H-B).
    *   If I remove K: M can still reach B via E (e.g., M-E-B) or L (e.g., M-L-E-B).
    *   If I remove E: M can still reach B via K (e.g., M-K-X-G-H-B).
    *   If I remove H: M can still reach B via E (e.g., M-E-B).
    *   If I remove C: M can still reach B via E (e.g., M-E-B) or H (e.g., M-K-X-G-H-B).
    *   If I remove N: M can still reach B via E (e.g., M-E-B) or H (e.g., M-K-X-G-H-B).
    *   If I remove G: M can still reach B via E (e.g., M-E-B).
    Since removing any single node does not disconnect M and B, the minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 2.

6.  **Systematic Analysis of Paths and Cut Sets of Size 2:**
    Let's categorize all paths from M to B based on their initial step from M:

    *   **Group 1: Paths starting M-L-...**
        *   M-L-E-... All paths from M through L must pass through E. So, if E is removed, all paths in this group are broken.

    *   **Group 2: Paths starting M-E-...**
        *   M-E-B
        *   M-E-C-B, M-E-C-N-B, M-E-C-O-B, M-E-C-O-N-B
        *   M-E-F-G-H-B, M-E-F-X-G-H-B, M-E-F-X-K-G-H-B
        All these paths rely on node E.

    *   **Group 3: Paths starting M-K-...**
        *   M-K-X-G-H-B
        *   M-K-F-G-H-B
        *   M-K-X-F-G-H-B
        *   M-K-F-E-... (These paths eventually pass through E, so they are covered by Group 2 if E is not removed, or broken if E is removed).

    From this analysis, it's clear that all paths from M to B either pass through node E, or they pass through the sequence of nodes K -> ... -> G -> H -> B.

7.  **Formulating a Minimal Cut Set:**
    To disconnect M and B, we need to break both main "branches" of paths:
    *   **Branch A:** All paths that go through node E.
    *   **Branch B:** All paths that go through the K-G-H sequence.

    Let's try removing one node from each "branch":

    *   **Option 1: Remove E (from Branch A).**
        If E is removed, all paths in Group 1 (M-L-E-...) and Group 2 (M-E-...) are broken.
        The only remaining paths are those from Group 3 that do not rely on E (i.e., M-K-...-G-H-B). These are:
        *   M-K-X-G-H-B
        *   M-K-F-G-H-B
        *   M-K-X-F-G-H-B
        All these remaining paths converge through G and H to reach B. Therefore, removing either G or H will break all of them.
        So, a cut set of size 2 could be **[E, G]** or **[E, H]**.

    *   **Verification of [E, G]:**
        1.  Removing E breaks all paths M-L-E-..., M-E-..., and M-K-F-E-....
        2.  The only remaining paths are M-K-X-G-H-B, M-K-F-G-H-B, M-K-X-F-G-H-B.
        3.  Removing G breaks all these remaining paths, as G is a necessary intermediate node.
        Therefore, [E, G] successfully disconnects M and B.

    *   **Verification of [E, H]:**
        1.  Removing E breaks all paths M-L-E-..., M-E-..., and M-K-F-E-....
        2.  The only remaining paths are M-K-X-G-H-B, M-K-F-G-H-B, M-K-X-F-G-H-B.
        3.  Removing H breaks all these remaining paths, as H is a necessary intermediate node.
        Therefore, [E, H] successfully disconnects M and B.

Since we've established that a single node removal is insufficient, and we've found cut sets of size 2, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 2.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [E, G]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 2, 'cut_set': ['J', 'E']}

Image name: POGBa_task3_gt3_PAIR3
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 6
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The target nodes are M (black) and B (black). My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect M from B.

First, I identify all possible paths between M and B. To do this systematically, I consider the immediate neighbors of M and B.
Neighbors of M: E, L, K
Neighbors of B: E, H, C, N

I will try to find edge-disjoint paths between M and B. Finding the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths will give me the minimum number of edges required to cut all paths (Menger's Theorem).

Let's identify potential edge-disjoint paths:

1.  **Path 1 (P1): M - E - B**
    Edges: (M, E), (E, B)

2.  **Path 2 (P2): M - K - X - G - H - B**
    Edges: (M, K), (K, X), (X, G), (G, H), (H, B)

3.  **Path 3 (P3): M - L - E - C - B**
    Edges: (M, L), (L, E), (E, C), (C, B)

Now, I check if these three paths are edge-disjoint (i.e., they do not share any common edges):
*   Edges in P1: {(M, E), (E, B)}
*   Edges in P2: {(M, K), (K, X), (X, G), (G, H), (H, B)}
*   Edges in P3: {(M, L), (L, E), (E, C), (C, B)}

Upon inspection, all edges in P1 are distinct from edges in P2 and P3. Similarly, all edges in P2 are distinct from P1 and P3, and all edges in P3 are distinct from P1 and P2. Therefore, these three paths are indeed edge-disjoint.

Since I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between M and B, according to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect M from B is at least 3.

Next, I need to find a cut set of 3 edges that successfully disconnects M from B. I will look for "bottleneck" edges that cut these three paths.

Consider the following set of edges:
*   (E, B) - This edge cuts Path 1 (M - E - B).
*   (G, H) - This edge cuts Path 2 (M - K - X - G - H - B).
*   (E, C) - This edge cuts Path 3 (M - L - E - C - B).

Let's verify if removing these three edges [(E, B), (E, C), (G, H)] disconnects M from B:

1.  **Paths starting M - E ...**:
    *   M - E - B: This path is broken by removing (E, B).
    *   M - E - C - B: This path is broken by removing (E, C).
    *   M - E - C - N - B: This path is broken by removing (E, C).

2.  **Paths starting M - L ...**:
    *   M - L - E - B: This path must pass through E. If E goes to B directly, it's broken by (E, B).
    *   M - L - E - C - B: This path must pass through E and C. It's broken by (E, C).
    *   M - L - E - C - N - B: This path must pass through E and C. It's broken by (E, C).

3.  **Paths starting M - K ...**:
    *   M - K - X - G - H - B: This path is broken by removing (G, H).
    *   M - K - F - G - H - B: This path is broken by removing (G, H).

All identified paths from M to B are broken by the removal of these three edges. Since we found 3 edge-disjoint paths, and we found a cut set of 3 edges that breaks all paths, this is the minimum number of edges.

An alternative minimal cut set of size 3 would be to remove all edges directly connected to M: [(M, E), (M, L), (M, K)]. This would also isolate M from the rest of the graph.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(E, B), (E, C), (G, H)]

- Node H is connected to C, B, I.
- Node F is connected to P, E.
- Node K is connected to F, X (and G).
- Node X is connected to F, K (and G).

Notice that nodes K and X are only connected to G, F, and each other. This means that any path from N to K or N to X (without going through G) must necessarily pass through F.
- If a path goes N -> ... -> K -> G: To reach K from N, it must come via F or X. If it comes via X, then N -> ... -> X -> K -> G. To reach X from N, it must come via F or K. If it comes via K, it creates a cycle (N -> ... -> K -> X -> K -> G). So, effectively, any path to K or X from N must pass through F.
- Therefore, all paths from N to G must pass through either H or F.

Let's verify this claim by examining all possible paths from N to G:
1.  Paths through H:
    *   N - C - H - G
    *   N - B - C - H - G
    *   N - O - B - C - H - G
    *   N - C - H - I - P - F - G (uses H and F)
    *   N - B - C - H - I - P - F - G (uses H and F)
    *   N - O - B - C - H - I - P - F - G (uses H and F)
2.  Paths through F (that don't necessarily use H):
    *   N - Y - O - P - F - G
    *   N - O - P - F - G
    *   N - O - B - E - P - F - G
    *   N - O - B - E - M - J - P - F - G
    *   N - C - I - P - F - G
    *   N - C - B - E - P - F - G
    *   N - C - B - E - M - J - P - F - G
    *   N - C - B - O - P - F - G
    *   N - B - E - P - F - G
    *   N - B - E - M - J - P - F - G
    *   N - B - O - P - F - G
    *   N - B - C - I - P - F - G

From this analysis, it is clear that every path from N to G must pass through either node H or node F.
- If a path uses H, it is broken by removing H.
- If a path uses F, it is broken by removing F.
- If a path uses K or X to reach G, it must have first passed through F to reach K or X from N (as established above). So, removing F also breaks these paths.

Since a single node removal is not sufficient, and we have found a cut set of size 2, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 2.

First, let's list the direct neighbors of E and J:
*   Neighbors of E: F, L, M, I, B
*   Neighbors of J: M, D, P, I

Let's consider the edges directly connected to J: (J, M), (J, D), (J, P), (J, I). If we remove all four of these edges, J would be completely isolated, thus disconnected from E. This would be a cut of size 4.

Let's try to find if a smaller cut (e.g., 1, 2, or 3 edges) is possible.

**Attempting a cut of 1 or 2 edges:**
*   There are multiple distinct paths between E and J (e.g., E-M-J and E-I-J). Therefore, removing a single edge is not sufficient.
*   Removing two edges like (M, J) and (I, J) would cut two direct paths. However, other paths still exist, such as E-B-C-O-P-J or E-L-M-J. So, 2 edges are not enough.

**Attempting a cut of 3 edges:**
Let's consider the nodes that are directly connected to J: M, I, P, D.
If we remove the edges (J, M), (J, I), and (J, P) (3 edges), J would still be connected to D.
Now, we need to check if E can still reach D. If E can reach D, and D is connected to J, then E is not disconnected from J.
Let's list paths from E to D:
1.  E - I - P - D
2.  E - B - C - O - P - D
3.  E - B - H - I - P - D
4.  E - F - G - H - I - P - D
5.  E - F - G - H - B - I - P - D
6.  E - F - G - H - B - C - O - P - D

All these paths from E to D pass through node P and then use the edge (P, D). Since E can reach D via P, and D is connected to J, removing only (J, M), (J, I), and (J, P) is not sufficient to disconnect E from J.

This implies that to disconnect J from E, we must not only cut its connections to M, I, and P, but also ensure that E cannot reach D (which is still connected to J). The most direct way to prevent E from reaching D (given that all paths from E to D go through P) is to remove the edge (P, D).

**Proposed Cut Set:** {(J, M), (J, I), (J, P), (P, D)}
Let's verify this cut set of 4 edges:
1.  **Remove (J, M):** This breaks paths like E-M-J and E-L-M-J.
2.  **Remove (J, I):** This breaks paths like E-I-J, E-B-H-I-J, E-F-G-H-I-J, etc.
3.  **Remove (J, P):** This breaks paths like E-I-P-J, E-B-C-O-P-J, E-F-G-H-B-C-O-P-J, etc.
After removing these three edges, J is now only connected to D.
4.  **Remove (P, D):** With (J, M), (J, I), and (J, P) already removed, J is only connected to D. By removing (P, D), we ensure that D is now only connected to J (as D's only other connection was to P).
    Now, we must verify that E cannot reach D. As identified above, all paths from E to D go through P. Since the edge (P, D) is removed, E can no longer reach D.
    Therefore, E cannot reach D, and D is only connected to J. This means E is completely disconnected from J.

**Minimality Check:**
We have established that 3 edges are not sufficient because, after removing any three edges from the set {(J, M), (J, I), (J, P), (D, J), (P, D)}, there would still be a path. For example:
*   If we remove (J, M), (J, I), (J, P), the path E-I-P-D-J remains (since (P,D) and (D,J) are still present).
*   If we remove (J, M), (J, I), (P, D), the path E-I-P-J remains (since (J,P) is still present).
*   If we remove (J, M), (J, P), (P, D), the path E-I-J remains (since (J,I) is still present).
*   If we remove (J, I), (J, P), (P, D), the path E-M-J remains (since (J,M) is still present).

Since each of the 4 edges in the proposed cut set is necessary to break all paths, and we have shown that 3 edges are not enough, the minimum number of edges to remove is 4.

4.  **Identify critical nodes for disconnection:**
    *   We have identified two main "bottlenecks" or "chokepoints" for paths from B to M:
        *   Node E: It's part of the direct path B -> E -> M, and also part of paths from P (P -> E -> M).
        *   Node P: All paths from B (except B-E-M) must pass through P.

5.  **Test for a single-node cut:**
    *   If we remove E: The path B -> E -> M is broken. However, paths like B -> C -> O -> P -> J -> M still exist. So, E alone is not enough.
    *   If we remove P: All paths that go through P are broken (e.g., B -> C -> O -> P -> J -> M). However, the direct path B -> E -> M still exists. So, P alone is not enough.
    *   If we remove J: Paths like B -> E -> M still exist. So, J alone is not enough.
    *   If we remove L: Paths like B -> E -> M and B -> C -> O -> P -> J -> M still exist. So, L alone is not enough.
    *   No single node removal can disconnect B and M. Therefore, the minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 2.

6.  **Test for a two-node cut:**
    *   **Option 1: Remove E and J.**
        *   Removing E breaks the direct path B -> E -> M.
        *   After removing E, M is only connected to L and J. L is only connected to M (and the removed E), so it cannot form a path to B. Thus, any remaining path to M must go through J.
        *   As established in step 3, all paths from B (not using E) must pass through P. From P, the only remaining way to M is via J (since E is removed).
        *   Therefore, removing J will break all paths of the form B -> ... -> P -> J -> M.
        *   This set [E, J] successfully disconnects B and M.

    *   **Option 2: Remove E and P.**
        *   Removing E breaks the direct path B -> E -> M.
        *   Removing P breaks all paths that go through P. Since all paths from B (not using E) must pass through P, removing P will break all these alternative paths (e.g., B -> C -> O -> P -> J -> M).
        *   This set [E, P] also successfully disconnects B and M.

Since we found a cut set of size 2, and we've determined that a single node removal is insufficient, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 2.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [E, J] or [E, P]

    *   Paths to J: G - K - J - I still exists.
    *   Paths to P: G - F - M - L - E - B - N - O - P - I still exists.

    *   Paths to P: G - F - M - L - E - B - N - O - P - I still exists.
    *   Paths to C: G - F - M - L - E - B - C - I still exists.
    Not sufficient.

Conclusion: Two nodes are not sufficient. The minimum number of nodes to remove is at least 3.

*   Paths to J: G - K - J - I, G - F - X - K - J - I

To break all paths to P and C that go through the "lower" part of the graph (via B, N, O), I can remove B.

Let's try the cut set **[H, J, B]**:
1.  **Removing H:** Breaks all paths that directly use H (e.g., G-H-I) and paths that pass through H (e.g., G-H-B-C-I).
2.  **Removing J:** Breaks all paths that use J as an entry point to I. This includes G-K-J-I, G-F-X-K-J-I, and also paths to P that pass through J (G-K-J-D-P-I, G-F-X-K-J-D-P-I).

    *   G - F - M - L - E - B - C - I (broken by B)

    *   G - F - M - L - E - B - N - O - C - I (broken by B)

        *   Intermediate node: O.
    *   **Path 2:** P - J - I - C - N

    *   **Node H:** For paths like F -> G -> H -> I -> J, F -> K -> G -> H -> B -> ... -> J, F -> X -> K -> G -> H -> I -> J, etc.

5.  **Formulate a Cut Set:** If we remove both node E and node H, all paths from F to J will be severed:
    *   Removing E breaks all paths that start F -> E -> ...
    *   Removing H breaks all paths that start F -> G -> H -> ... (which covers all paths that start F -> G -> ... because G must go through H to reach J's side).
    Therefore, the set {E, H} is a cut set. The size of this cut set is 2.

6.  **Verify Minimum Size:**
    *   **Can a single node disconnect F and J?**
        *   If we remove E, paths through G and H still exist (e.g., F-G-H-I-J).
        *   If we remove H, paths through E still exist (e.g., F-E-M-J).
        *   If we remove G, paths through E still exist (e.g., F-E-M-J).
        *   Removing any other single node (e.g., I, M, P, B, C) would leave multiple alternative paths intact. For example, removing I still leaves paths like F-E-M-J or F-G-H-B-N-C-P-J.
    *   Since no single node can disconnect F and J, the minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 2.

7.  **Conclusion:** Since we found a cut set of size 2 ({E, H}) and confirmed that a single node is insufficient, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 2.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [E, H]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 2, 'cut_set': ['E', 'H']}

Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are H (black) and N (black). My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect H from N.

1.  **Identify direct connections to H and N:**
    *   Neighbors of H: G, B, I
    *   Neighbors of N: B, C, Y, O

2.  **Identify potential "entry points" to N from H's side:**
    All paths from H to N must eventually pass through one of N's neighbors (B, C, Y, O).
    *   Node B is connected to H and N.
    *   Node C is connected to N.
    *   Node O is connected to N.
    *   Node Y is connected to N and O. Any path from H to Y would have to go through N or O first.

3.  **Look for edge-disjoint paths:**
    To determine the minimum number of edges to remove (the edge connectivity), I can try to find the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between H and N. By Menger's Theorem, this number is equal to the minimum number of edges in an edge cut.

    Let's try to find edge-disjoint paths:
    *   **Path 1:** H - B - N
        Edges: (H, B), (B, N)

    *   **Path 2:** H - I - C - N
        Edges: (H, I), (I, C), (C, N)
        This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1, as (H, I) is different from (H, B), and (I, C), (C, N) are different from (B, N).

    *   **Path 3:** H - G - K - J - P - O - N
        Edges: (H, G), (G, K), (K, J), (J, P), (P, O), (O, N)
        This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2. The starting edge (H, G) is distinct from (H, B) and (H, I). The ending edge (O, N) is distinct from (B, N) and (C, N). All intermediate edges are also distinct.

    I have found three edge-disjoint paths between H and N. According to Menger's Theorem, this means that at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect H and N.

4.  **Find a cut set of size 3:**
    Since we know the minimum is at least 3, I need to find a set of 3 edges whose removal disconnects H and N.

    Consider the edges directly connected to N that serve as "gateways" for paths from H:
    *   (B, N): Cuts Path 1 (H-B-N) and any other path going through B to N (e.g., H-G-F-E-B-N).
    *   (C, N): Cuts Path 2 (H-I-C-N) and any other path going through C to N (e.g., H-B-C-N).
    *   (O, N): Cuts Path 3 (H-G-K-J-P-O-N) and any other path going through O to N (e.g., H-I-J-P-O-N, H-I-C-O-N).

    If we remove these three edges: [(B, N), (C, N), (O, N)]:
    *   All paths from H that reach N via B are broken by (B, N).
    *   All paths from H that reach N via C are broken by (C, N).
    *   All paths from H that reach N via O are broken by (O, N).
    *   Node Y is connected to N and O. Since (O, N) is removed, Y can no longer serve as a bridge from H to N. H cannot reach Y without passing through N or O.

    Therefore, removing these three edges successfully disconnects H from N.

5.  **Conclusion:**
    Since we found 3 edge-disjoint paths, the minimum number of edges to remove is at least 3. We also found a cut set of 3 edges that achieves the disconnection. Thus, 3 is the minimum.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(B, N), (C, N), (O, N)]

    *   J-I-H-X-K-F (via X then K)
    *   J-I-H-X-F (via X directly to F)

2.  **Apply Menger's Theorem (Edge Version):** The minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect two nodes is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between them. Let's try to find edge-disjoint paths.

    *   **Path 1 (P1): J - M - L - E - F**
        Edges: {(J,M), (M,L), (L,E), (E,F)}

    *   **Path 2 (P2): J - I - H - F**
        Edges: {(J,I), (I,H), (H,F)}
        (P1 and P2 are edge-disjoint as they use different initial edges from J and different intermediate nodes/edges.)

    *   **Path 3 (P3): J - D - P - O - N - C - H - X - K - F**
        Edges: {(J,D), (D,P), (P,O), (O,N), (N,C), (C,H), (H,X), (X,K), (K,F)}

        -   Initial edges from J: (J,D) is distinct from (J,M) and (J,I).
        -   Intermediate edges: All edges in P3 are distinct from those in P1 and P2. For example, (H,X) in P3 is distinct from (I,H) and (H,F) in P2. (K,F) is distinct from (E,F) in P1 and (H,F) in P2.
        Therefore, P1, P2, and P3 are three edge-disjoint paths.

    Since there are at least 3 edge-disjoint paths, the minimum number of edges to remove is at least 3.

3.  **Attempt to find a cut set of size 3:**
    To disconnect J and F, we must cut all paths.
    Let's try to cut one edge from each of the three edge-disjoint paths found above.
    If we choose the final edges: [(E,F), (H,F), (K,F)].
    *   (E,F) cuts P1 and all other paths through E.
    *   (H,F) cuts P2.
    *   (K,F) cuts P3.

    *   J-I-H-X-G-F (uses (X,G) and (G,F))

4.  **Find a cut set of size 4:**

    We have found a valid cut set of 4 edges.
    Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 4.

    *   M - L - E - B (This path also uses E, which is a neighbor of M and B)
    *   M - L - F - X - K - G - H - B
3.  **Paths through J:**
    *   M - J - P - O - N - B
    *   M - J - D - P - O - N - B
    *   M - J - P - I - C - B
    *   M - J - D - P - I - C - B
    *   M - J - P - I - H - B
    *   M - J - D - P - I - H - B

To disconnect M from B, I need to break all these paths.

**Strategy 1: Isolate one of the target nodes.**
*   **Isolating M:** M has three outgoing edges: (M, L), (M, E), and (M, J). If I remove all three of these edges, M will be completely disconnected from the rest of the graph, and thus from B. This would require removing 3 edges.
    *   Cut Set: [(M, L), (M, E), (M, J)]
*   **Isolating B:** B has four outgoing edges: (B, N), (B, C), (B, H), and (B, E). Removing all four would disconnect B. This would require removing 4 edges.

Since the goal is to find the *minimum* number of edges, isolating M (3 edges) is a better candidate than isolating B (4 edges).

**Strategy 2: Find a smaller cut set by cutting "bottlenecks" in the middle of the graph.**
Let's try to see if a cut set of 1 or 2 edges is possible.

*   **Can 1 edge disconnect M and B?** No, because there are multiple independent paths. For example, removing (M, E) still leaves paths through L and J. Removing (E, B) still leaves paths through N, C, and H.
*   **Can 2 edges disconnect M and B?**
    *   Consider removing two edges directly connected to M, e.g., (M, E) and (M, L). Paths starting with M-J (e.g., M-J-P-O-N-B) would still exist. So, 2 edges connected to M are not enough.
    *   Consider removing two edges directly connected to B, e.g., (B, E) and (B, N). Paths through C (e.g., M-J-P-I-C-B) and H (e.g., M-L-F-X-K-G-H-B) would still exist. So, 2 edges connected to B are not enough.
    *   Consider removing two edges "in the middle," e.g., (P, O) and (P, I). Paths M-E-B, M-L-E-B, and M-L-F-X-K-G-H-B would still exist. So, 2 edges in the middle are not enough.
    *   Consider removing one edge from M's side and one from B's side, e.g., (M, E) and (B, N). Paths like M-L-E-B, M-J-P-I-C-B, and M-L-F-X-K-G-H-B would still exist.

Since I've demonstrated that 2 edges are insufficient to disconnect M and B, the minimum number of edges must be at least 3.

As established in Strategy 1, removing the three edges directly connected to M (M, L), (M, E), and (M, J) completely isolates M from the rest of the graph, thereby disconnecting it from B. This is a valid cut set of size 3.

Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(M, L), (M, E), (M, J)]

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are I and E. My goal is to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect I and E, without removing I or E themselves. This is a minimum vertex cut problem. According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of vertices whose removal disconnects two non-adjacent vertices (s and t) is equal to the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths between s and t.

I will identify vertex-disjoint paths between I and E. A path is vertex-disjoint if it shares no intermediate nodes with other paths.

1.  **Path 1: I - P - E**
    *   This is a direct path through node P.
    *   Intermediate node: {P}

2.  **Path 2: I - J - M - E**
    *   This path uses nodes J and M.
    *   Intermediate nodes: {J, M}. This set is disjoint from {P}.

3.  **Path 3: I - C - B - E**
    *   This path uses nodes C and B.
    *   Intermediate nodes: {C, B}. This set is disjoint from {P} and {J, M}.

I have identified three vertex-disjoint paths between I and E:
*   Path 1: I - P - E
*   Path 2: I - J - M - E
*   Path 3: I - C - B - E

Since there are 3 vertex-disjoint paths, the minimum number of nodes that must be removed to disconnect I and E is at least 3.

Now, I need to find a set of 3 nodes whose removal disconnects I and E. To do this, I can select one intermediate node from each of the three disjoint paths.

*   From Path 1 (I - P - E), I can choose to remove node P.
*   From Path 2 (I - J - M - E), I can choose to remove node J (or M).
*   From Path 3 (I - C - B - E), I can choose to remove node C (or B).

Let's choose the cut set [P, J, C]. I will now verify that removing these three nodes breaks all possible paths between I and E.

Consider all possible paths from I to E:
*   **Paths starting with I -> P:**
    *   I - P - E (Broken by removing P)
    *   I - P - J - M - E (Broken by removing P and J)
    *   I - P - O - N - C - B - E (Broken by removing P and C)
    *   I - P - O - Y - N - C - B - E (Broken by removing P and C)
    *   I - P - F ... (F does not lead to E)
*   **Paths starting with I -> J (that don't immediately go to P):**
    *   I - J - M - E (Broken by removing J)
    *   I - J - P - E (Broken by removing J and P)
*   **Paths starting with I -> C (that don't immediately go to P):**

Let's list the direct neighbors of B: H, E, I, C, N.

Let's consider the set of nodes that are "closer" to H and form a connected component with H, and the set of nodes that are "closer" to B and form a connected component with B.

Consider the component containing H, and all nodes that can be reached from H without passing through B or any node that is clearly on the "B-side" of the graph.
Let's define a partition of the graph into two sets of nodes, S and T, such that H is in S and B is in T. The minimum cut will be the set of edges that connect a node in S to a node in T.

Let's start with H in set S.

B is the target, so it must be in T.

Now, let's define T as all other nodes:
T = {B, E, I, C, N, O, Y, J, P, D, L, M}

1.  **Edges from H (in S) to T:**

    *   K is connected to X, F, G. All these are in S. No edges from K to T.

    *   If I remove H: Paths through F (e.g., G-F-E-B-N) would still exist.
    *   If I remove F: Paths through H (e.g., G-H-B-N) would still exist.
    *   If I remove B: Paths through C (e.g., G-H-I-C-N) would still exist.

    *   If I remove any other single node (e.g., I, P, E, M, J, O, X, K, L, D), there would always be alternative paths. For example, removing I would leave G-H-B-N and G-F-E-B-N.
    *   Therefore, a single node removal is not sufficient to disconnect G and N. The minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 2.

        Both of these path structures converge at node O before reaching P via edge (O, P) or via Y and then P. The edge **(O, P)** is a critical link for all paths from E to P. If (O, P) is removed, P becomes disconnected from the entire "E-side" of the graph.

    *   **Paths through D:**
        Node D is only connected to P and J. If P is disconnected from the "E-side" (by removing (O, P)), then there is no way for E to reach D (since D's only connection to the rest of the graph, besides J, is P). Therefore, any path E-...-D-J is also broken if P is disconnected from E.

    *   **(M, J)**: Cuts all paths from E to J via M.
    *   **(I, J)**: Cuts all paths from E to J via I.
    *   **(O, P)**: Cuts all paths from E to J via P (and consequently via D, as D relies on P for connection to E).

2.  **Explore paths between N and P:** I will systematically list paths from N to P to understand the connectivity. To determine the minimum cut, I will look for node-disjoint paths. Menger's theorem states that the maximum number of node-disjoint paths between two non-adjacent nodes is equal to the minimum number of nodes needed to disconnect them.

    Let's find node-disjoint paths (paths that share no intermediate nodes):
    *   **Path 1:** N - O - P
        (Nodes: N, O, P)

    *   **Path 2:** N - C - I - P
        (Nodes: N, C, I, P)
        This path is node-disjoint from Path 1 (O is not C or I).

    *   **Path 3:** N - B - E - M - J - P
        (Nodes: N, B, E, M, J, P)
        This path is node-disjoint from Path 1 (O is not B, E, M, J).
        This path is node-disjoint from Path 2 (C, I are not B, E, M, J).

    I have found three node-disjoint paths between N and P:
    1.  N - O - P
    2.  N - C - I - P
    3.  N - B - E - M - J - P

3.  **Determine the minimum cut size:** Since there are at least three node-disjoint paths between N and P, according to Menger's theorem, the minimum number of nodes that must be removed to disconnect N and P is at least 3.

4.  **Find a cut set of size 3:** To disconnect N and P, I need to remove at least one node from each of these three disjoint paths (excluding N and P).
    *   From Path 1 (N - O - P), I can remove node O.
    *   From Path 2 (N - C - I - P), I can remove node C (or I). Let's choose C.
    *   From Path 3 (N - B - E - M - J - P), I can remove node B (or E, M, J). Let's choose B.

    *   If **O** is removed: The direct path N-O-P is broken.
    *   If **C** is removed: The path N-C-I-P is broken.

    *   Node N is now only connected to node Y.
    *   Node Y is only connected to N and has no other connections to the rest of the graph that could lead to P.
    *   Therefore, N is completely disconnected from P.

5.  **Conclusion:** Since I found a cut set of size 3, and I previously determined that the minimum cut size must be at least 3, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

This gives a potential cut set of size 3: [(G, H), (I, H), (B, H)].

Next, I need to determine if this is the minimum number of edges. To do this, I will look for edge-disjoint paths between E and H. If I can find 'k' edge-disjoint paths, then by Menger's Theorem, at least 'k' edges must be removed to disconnect the two nodes.

Let's identify edge-disjoint paths from E to H:
1.  **Path 1: E - G - H**
    Edges: (E, G), (G, H)

2.  **Path 2: E - B - H**

    Edges: (E, M), (M, J), (J, P), (P, I), (I, H)

*   Path 2 uses (E, B) and (B, H).
*   Path 3 uses (E, M), (M, J), (J, P), (P, I), and (I, H).

All edges in these three paths are distinct. Therefore, these are three edge-disjoint paths between E and H.

According to Menger's Theorem, if there are 'k' edge-disjoint paths between two nodes, then at least 'k' edges must be removed to disconnect them. Since I found 3 edge-disjoint paths, the minimum number of edges to remove is at least 3.

Since I have identified a cut set of 3 edges ([(G, H), (I, H), (B, H)]) that successfully disconnects E and H, and I have proven that at least 3 edges are required, this cut set is indeed minimal.

The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect the two black nodes, M and N. This is equivalent to finding the minimum cut between M and N. By Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges in an edge cut is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between the two nodes.

1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The target nodes are M and N.

2.  **Explore paths from M to N:** I will systematically search for edge-disjoint paths between M and N.

    *   **Path 1 (P1):** Let's start from M and try to find a path to N.
        M is connected to J, E, L.
        N is connected to B, C, O, Y. (Note: Y is only connected to N and O, and O is connected to P, C, N. Y does not seem to be a critical node for paths from M to N, as any path through Y would have to go N-Y-O-P... or N-Y-O-C... which is not a path from M to N. So, Y is a dead end for paths from M to N).

        Let's try a path through J:
        M - J - P - O - N
        Edges in P1: {(M, J), (J, P), (P, O), (O, N)}

    *   **Path 2 (P2):** Now, I need to find a path from M to N that does not use any of the edges from P1.

        Edges in P2: {(M, E), (E, H), (H, B), (B, N)}

        - (J, P) vs (E, H) - distinct
        - (P, O) vs (H, B) - distinct
        - (O, N) vs (B, N) - distinct
        Yes, P1 and P2 are edge-disjoint. This means we need to remove at least 2 edges.

    *   **Path 3 (P3):** Now, I need to find a third path from M to N that does not use any of the edges from P1 or P2.
        The remaining edge from M not used is (M, L).
        The remaining edge to N not used is (N, C).

        Let's try to construct a path using (M, L) and ending with (N, C):
        M - L - E ... (L is connected to E)
        (M, L) is not in P1 or P2.
        (L, E) is not in P1 or P2.

            (M, L) vs (M, J) - distinct
            (L, E) vs (J, P) - distinct
            (E, G) vs (P, O) - distinct

            (H, I) vs (O, N) - distinct

            (C, N) vs (B, N) - distinct
            P3 is edge-disjoint from P2.

        Path: G - H - B - N - C - I. This path still exists because (G, H) is not removed.
        Therefore, removing these 3 edges is not sufficient.

    *   **Scenario 3: Remove (I, J), (I, C), (I, H).**
        The edge (I, P) remains. We need to check if there's any path from G to P using the remaining edges.
        Path: G - F - M - L - E - B - N - O - P - I. This path still exists because (I, P) is not removed.
        Path: G - H - B - N - O - P - I. This path still exists because (G, H) is not removed.
        Therefore, removing these 3 edges is not sufficient.

    *   **Scenario 4: Remove (I, P), (I, C), (I, H).**
        The edge (I, J) remains. We need to check if there's any path from G to J using the remaining edges.
        Path: G - K - J - I. This path still exists because (I, J) is not removed.
        Path: G - F - X - K - J - I. This path still exists because (I, J) is not removed.
        Therefore, removing these 3 edges is not sufficient.

    Since removing any 3 edges directly connected to I leaves at least one path, a cut of size 3 is not possible by targeting only I's direct connections. Similar logic applies if we try to remove 3 edges directly connected to G.

    Let's consider a mixed set of 3 edges, for example, (G, H), (I, J), (I, P).
    *   (G, H) cuts all paths from G that start with G-H.
    *   (I, J) cuts all paths to I that end with J-I.
    *   (I, P) cuts all paths to I that end with P-I.
    After removing these 3 edges:
    *   G can still reach F, X, K.
    *   I can still be reached from C, H. (Note: H is still connected to I via (H, I)).
    Since (G, H) is removed, G cannot reach H. So, paths to I must go through C.
    Remaining paths:
    *   G - F - M - L - E - B - N - C - I (still exists, as (I, C) is not removed)
    *   G - X - F - M - L - E - B - N - C - I (still exists)
    *   G - K - X - F - M - L - E - B - N - C - I (still exists)
    Therefore, removing (G, H), (I, J), (I, P) is not sufficient. We would need to remove (I, C) as well, making it 4 edges.

4.  **Conclusion on minimum cut size:**
    Based on the systematic exploration, it appears that any set of 3 edges will leave at least one path between G and I. Therefore, the minimum number of edges required to disconnect G and I is 4. The simplest way to achieve this is to remove all edges connected to one of the target nodes.

5.  **Select a minimal cut set:**
    Removing all edges connected to node I: (I, J), (I, P), (I, C), (I, H). This set has 4 edges and completely isolates I from the rest of the graph, thus disconnecting it from G.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 4, Cut Set: [(I, J), (I, P), (I, C), (I, H)]

The target nodes are B and H. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to ensure no path exists between B and H.

1.  **Identify all direct connections to H:**
    Node H is directly connected to three other nodes: B, G, and I.
    Therefore, any path from B to H must traverse one of these three edges: (B, H), (G, H), or (I, H).

2.  **Consider a cut set by removing all edges connected to H (except those within the B-H path itself):**
    If we remove the edges (B, H), (G, H), and (I, H), node H becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph. This means no path can exist between B and H.
    This cut set has 3 edges: `[(B, H), (G, H), (I, H)]`. This is a valid way to disconnect B and H.

3.  **Determine if a smaller cut (e.g., 2 edges) is possible:**
    To prove that 3 is the minimum, I need to show that it's impossible to disconnect B and H by removing only 1 or 2 edges.
    Let's try to find edge-disjoint paths between B and H. If I can find *k* edge-disjoint paths, then by Menger's Theorem, at least *k* edges must be removed to disconnect B and H.

    *   **Path 1:** B - H
        This is a direct path using the edge (B, H).

    *   **Path 2:** B - E - F - X - G - H
        This path uses edges: (B, E), (E, F), (F, X), (X, G), (G, H).
        This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1, as it does not use (B, H).

    *   **Path 3:** B - C - I - H
        This path uses edges: (B, C), (C, I), (I, H).
        This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1 (does not use (B, H)).
        This path is also edge-disjoint from Path 2 (e.g., (B, C) is different from (B, E); (C, I) is different from (E, F), etc.; (I, H) is different from (G, H)).

    Since I have identified three edge-disjoint paths between B and H, it means that at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect them.

4.  **Conclusion:**
    I found a cut set of 3 edges that disconnects B and H, and I've shown that it's impossible to do so with fewer than 3 edges by finding 3 edge-disjoint paths. Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(B, H), (G, H), (I, H)]

        *   The length of this path is 2 (M-O, O-Y).

    *   M is connected to L, E, F, and J.

    *   Edges: (E, B), (G, B), (H, B), (C, B), (N, B).

    *   If I remove all edges directly connected to B, B becomes isolated. This set includes (E, B), (G, B), (H, B), (C, B), (N, B). This is a cut set of 5 edges.
    *   Since 4 is less than 5, the minimum number of edges is at most 4.

    I will systematically explore combinations of 3 edges to see if they can disconnect M from B.
    *   **Attempt 1: Cut the "main branches" from M.**
        If I remove (M, E), (M, F), (M, J), the path M-L-E-B still exists. So, 3 edges are not enough.

        Consider the edges (E, B), (G, B), and (J, I).
        *   (E, B) cuts paths M-L-E-B and M-E-B.
        *   (G, B) cuts paths M-E-G-B, M-F-G-B, M-F-X-K-G-B, M-F-K-G-B.

        *   Remaining paths: M-J-P-O-C-B and M-J-P-O-Y-N-B (these paths use (J,P) and then connect to B via C or N). So, 3 edges are not enough.
        Consider the edges (E, B), (G, B), and (J, P).
        *   (E, B) cuts paths M-L-E-B and M-E-B.
        *   (G, B) cuts paths M-E-G-B, M-F-G-B, M-F-X-K-G-B, M-F-K-G-B.

        *   Remaining paths: M-J-I-H-B and M-J-I-C-B (these paths use (J,I) and then connect to B via H or C). So, 3 edges are not enough.

5.  **Confirm the minimum using Menger's Theorem (finding edge-disjoint paths):**
    If I can find 4 edge-disjoint paths between M and B, then by Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove is at least 4.
    Let's identify 4 edge-disjoint paths:
    *   **Path 1:** M - E - B. Edges: {(M,E), (E,B)}
    *   **Path 2:** M - F - G - B. Edges: {(M,F), (F,G), (G,B)}
    *   **Path 3:** M - J - I - C - B. Edges: {(M,J), (J,I), (I,C), (C,B)}
    *   **Path 4:** M - L - E - G - H - B. Edges: {(M,L), (L,E), (E,G), (G,H), (H,B)}

    Let's verify that these 4 paths are indeed edge-disjoint:
    *   The first edges from M are (M,E), (M,F), (M,J), (M,L) - all distinct.
    *   The last edges to B are (E,B), (G,B), (C,B), (H,B) - all distinct.
    *   Intermediate edges:

        *   (J,I) and (I,C) in P3 are distinct from (L,E), (E,G), (G,H), (F,G).
        *   (L,E), (E,G), (G,H) in P4 are distinct from (F,G), (J,I), (I,C).
    All edges used in these four paths are unique to their respective paths. Therefore, these are 4 edge-disjoint paths.

6.  **Conclusion:**
    Since there are 4 edge-disjoint paths, the minimum cut must be at least 4. As we found a cut set of 4 edges (e.g., removing all direct connections from M), this is the minimum.

Let's find node-disjoint paths from E to J:

1.  **Path 1: E - M - J**
    *   Intermediate node: M

2.  **Path 2: E - H - I - J**
    *   Intermediate nodes: H, I
    *   This path is node-disjoint from Path 1 (M is not H or I).

    *   Intermediate nodes: L, N, O, P
    *   This path is node-disjoint from Path 1 (M is not L, N, O, P).
    *   This path is node-disjoint from Path 2 (H, I are not L, N, O, P).

If we remove M, I, and P, then J will only be connected to D.
Node D is only connected to J and P. Since P is removed, D becomes isolated from the rest of the graph.
Therefore, if M, I, and P are removed, J will be completely disconnected from E and any other part of the graph that could lead to E.

*   Removing M breaks Path 1 (E - M - J) and any other path through M (e.g., E - L - M - J).
*   Removing I breaks Path 2 (E - H - I - J) and any other path through I (e.g., E - H - B - C - I - J).
*   Removing P breaks Path 3 (E - L - N - O - P - J) and any other path through P (e.g., E - H - B - C - O - P - J).

Since we have found 3 node-disjoint paths, and a cut set of size 3, this is the minimum number of nodes to remove.

1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are G and I. I need to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect G from I. I cannot remove G or I.

    *   **Direct neighbors of G:** F, K, X, H.
    *   **Direct neighbors of I:** H, C, J, P.

3.  **Analyze paths through H:**
    *   G - H - I (Direct path)
    *   G - H - B - C - I
    *   G - H - B - N - C - I
    *   G - H - E - M - J - I
    *   G - H - E - M - J - P - I

4.  **Analyze paths not directly through H from G:** These paths must start from G and go through F, K, or X.
    *   If G -> F (or G -> K -> F, or G -> X -> F):
        *   From F, the path can go to E.
        *   From E, the path can go to H (which then leads to I, e.g., G-F-E-H-I).
        *   From E, the path can go to M. From M, it can go to J. From J, it can go to I (G-F-E-M-J-I) or P. From P, it can go to I (G-F-E-M-J-P-I).

5.  **Identify critical nodes:**
    Upon reviewing all paths, I observe a crucial pattern:
    *   Any path from G to I either passes through node **H**.
    *   OR, if it doesn't pass through H, it must go through G's other neighbors (F, K, X). From F, K, or X, the only way to proceed towards I without going through H is via node **E**. From E, the paths then continue through M, J, and/or P to reach I.

    Therefore, every single path from G to I must pass through either node H or node E.

6.  **Test for a single-node cut:**
    *   If I remove H, paths like G-F-E-M-J-I still exist. So, H alone is not enough.
    *   If I remove E, paths like G-H-I still exist. So, E alone is not enough.
    *   This confirms that a single node removal is insufficient. The minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 2.

7.  **Test for a two-node cut:**
    Based on the observation in step 5, if all paths must pass through H or E, then removing both H and E should disconnect G and I.
    *   **Remove H:** All paths that directly use H (e.g., G-H-I, G-H-B-C-I, G-H-E-M-J-I) are broken. Any remaining path from G to I must now go through G's other neighbors (F, K, X) and then through E.
    *   **Remove E (in addition to H):** All paths that previously relied on E (e.g., G-F-E-M-J-I, G-K-F-E-M-J-P-I) are now broken. Since H is also removed, there are no alternative routes through H.

    Thus, removing both H and E successfully disconnects G and I. This forms a cut set of size 2.

8.  **Verify minimality:** Since a single node removal was not sufficient, and we found a cut set of size 2, this is the minimum possible number of nodes to remove.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [H, E]

    Consider the set of nodes S = {F, K, X, G, E, L, M}.

    *   From F: All connections (F, K), (F, X), (F, G), (F, E) are to nodes within S.

    The edges that connect the set S = {F, K, X, G, E, L, M} to the rest of the graph (which includes J) are:
    *   (G, H)
    *   (E, B)
    *   (M, J)

    If these three edges are removed, there will be no path from any node in S (including F) to any node outside S (including J). Therefore, F will be completely disconnected from J. This gives a cut set of 3 edges.

5.  **Verify that this cut set disconnects all paths:**
    Let's check the major pathways from F to J:
    *   **Paths through M:** Any path from F to J that goes through M (e.g., F-E-M-J, F-E-L-M-J) will be cut by removing (M, J).
    *   **Paths through G and H:** Any path from F to J that goes through G and then H (e.g., F-G-H-I-J, F-G-H-B-C-O-P-J) will be cut by removing (G, H).
    *   **Paths through E and B:** Any path from F to J that goes through E and then B (e.g., F-E-B-H-I-J, F-E-B-C-O-P-J) will be cut by removing (E, B).
    Since all possible paths from F to J must use one of these three "exit" edges from the F-cluster, removing them will disconnect F from J.

6.  **Determine minimality:**
    We have found a cut set of 3 edges. To prove it's minimal, we need to show that no set of 2 edges can disconnect F from J.
    *   If we remove (G, H) and (E, B), the path F-E-M-J still exists.
    *   If we remove (G, H) and (M, J), the path F-E-B-C-I-J (or any path through E-B) still exists.
    *   If we remove (E, B) and (M, J), the path F-G-H-I-J (or any path through G-H) still exists.
    Since removing any two of these three edges leaves at least one path between F and J, a cut set of 2 edges is not possible.

Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

    *   Dequeue `(L, [L])`.
    *   Neighbors of L: E, M.
        *   Add `(E, [L, E])` to queue.
        *   Add `(M, [L, M])` to queue.
    *   Visited: `{L, E, M}`
    *   Current queue: `[(E, [L, E]), (M, [L, M])]`

        *   Neighbors of E: L (visited), M (visited), F, B.
        *   Add `(F, [L, E, F])` to queue.
        *   Add `(B, [L, E, B])` to queue.

        *   Neighbors of F: E (visited), X, K.
        *   Add `(X, [L, E, F, X])` to queue.
        *   Add `(K, [L, E, F, K])` to queue.
    *   Visited: `{L, E, M, F, B, J, X, K}`

        *   Neighbors of B: E (visited), N, H, C.
        *   Add `(N, [L, E, B, N])` to queue.
        *   Add `(H, [L, E, B, H])` to queue.
        *   Add `(C, [L, E, B, C])` to queue.
    *   Visited: `{L, E, M, F, B, J, X, K, N, H, C}`

        *   Add `(D, [L, M, J, D])` to queue.
    *   Visited: `{L, E, M, F, B, J, X, K, N, H, C, D}`
    *   Current queue: `[(X, [L, E, F, X]), (K, [L, E, F, K]), (N, [L, E, B, N]), (H, [L, E, B, H]), (C, [L, E, B, C]), (D, [L, M, J, D])]`

        *   Neighbors of X: F (visited), K (visited), G.
        *   Add `(G, [L, E, F, X, G])` to queue.
    *   Visited: `{..., G}`

        *   M-J-I-P-O-N

        *   M-J-P-I-C-N

3.  **Analyze Path Structure and Identify Bottlenecks:**
    I observe that all paths from M to N can be categorized into two main groups based on their initial divergence from M:
    *   **Group 1 (via L or E):** All paths that start with M-L-... or M-E-... eventually converge and must pass through node **H** to reach nodes B or C, which are connected to N. For example, M-L-E-H-B-N, M-E-H-C-N, M-E-F-G-H-B-N, etc. Node H is a critical point for all these paths. Alternatively, node E is also a critical point for all these paths, as L connects only to E, and E is the entry point to H for all these paths.
    *   **Group 2 (via J):** All paths that start with M-J-... must pass through node **J** itself. For example, M-J-I-C-N, M-J-P-O-N, etc. Node J is a critical point for all these paths.

4.  **Determine Minimum Cut Set:**
    *   Since there are two distinct groups of paths (one through H/E and another through J), to disconnect M and N, we must break at least one node from each group. This implies a minimum of two nodes must be removed.
    *   Let's consider removing one node from Group 1's critical points (H or E) and one node from Group 2's critical point (J).

    *   **Option A: Remove H and J.**
        *   Removing H will break all paths in Group 1 (M-L-E-H-...-N and M-E-H-...-N, including those through F and G).
        *   Removing J will break all paths in Group 2 (M-J-...-N).
        *   Therefore, removing [H, J] will disconnect M and N. This is a cut set of size 2.

    *   **Option B: Remove E and J.**
        *   Removing E will break all paths in Group 1 (M-L-E-...-N and M-E-...-N, including those through F and G).
        *   Removing J will break all paths in Group 2 (M-J-...-N).
        *   Therefore, removing [E, J] will disconnect M and N. This is also a cut set of size 2.

5.  **Verify Minimality:**
    *   We have identified two vertex-disjoint paths:
        *   Path 1: M - E - H - B - N
        *   Path 2: M - J - I - C - N
    *   These two paths share no intermediate nodes. According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes required to disconnect M and N (excluding M and N themselves) must be at least 2.
    *   Since we found cut sets of size 2, this is the minimum possible.

Both [H, J] and [E, J] are valid minimal cut sets. I will provide one of them.

            *   {H, N}: Cuts paths to K/X (via H) and paths to E (via N). But paths like J -> M -> E -> F remain (via M).

Since I have found a cut set of size 3, and I have demonstrated that there are 3 vertex-disjoint paths, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

3.  **Explore paths and potential bottlenecks:**
    I will look for edge-disjoint paths from X to C. According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove (min-cut) is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths (max-flow).

    Let's try to find three edge-disjoint paths:

    *   **Path 1 (P1): X - G - B - C**
        Edges: (X, G), (G, B), (B, C)

    *   **Path 2 (P2): X - F - E - L - M - J - P - O - C**
        Edges: (X, F), (F, E), (E, L), (L, M), (M, J), (J, P), (P, O), (O, C)

    *   **Path 3 (P3): X - K - G - H - I - C**
        Edges: (X, K), (K, G), (G, H), (H, I), (I, C)

4.  **Verify edge-disjointness of the paths:**
    *   Compare P1 and P2:
        P1 edges: {(X,G), (G,B), (B,C)}
        P2 edges: {(X,F), (F,E), (E,L), (L,M), (M,J), (J,P), (P,O), (O,C)}
        There are no common edges between P1 and P2.

    *   Compare P1 and P3:
        P1 edges: {(X,G), (G,B), (B,C)}
        P3 edges: {(X,K), (K,G), (G,H), (H,I), (I,C)}
        There are no common edges between P1 and P3. (X,G) is distinct from (X,K) and (K,G). (G,B) is distinct from (G,H). (B,C) is distinct from (H,I) and (I,C).

    *   Compare P2 and P3:
        P2 edges: {(X,F), (F,E), (E,L), (L,M), (M,J), (J,P), (P,O), (O,C)}
        P3 edges: {(X,K), (K,G), (G,H), (H,I), (I,C)}
        There are no common edges between P2 and P3.

    Since I have found three edge-disjoint paths between X and C, the minimum number of edges required to disconnect them must be at least 3.

5.  **Identify a cut set of size 3:**
    Now I need to find a set of 3 edges whose removal disconnects X from C. A good strategy is to look at the "exit" edges from the immediate neighborhood of X. The nodes F, K, G are directly connected to X. The edges that connect this cluster {X, F, K, G} to the rest of the graph are:
    *   (F, E) - This edge is part of P2.
    *   (G, B) - This edge is part of P1.
    *   (G, H) - This edge is part of P3.

    Let's propose the cut set: S = {(F, E), (G, B), (G, H)}.

6.  **Verify the proposed cut set:**
    *   If (F, E) is removed, all paths going through F and then E (like P2) are cut.
    *   If (G, B) is removed, all paths going through G and then B (like P1) are cut.
    *   If (G, H) is removed, all paths going through G and then H (like P3) are cut.

    Let's check all possible paths from X to C:
    *   Any path starting X-F must use (F, E) to reach the C-side (other than going to K or G, which are also in the X-cluster). Removing (F, E) cuts these paths.
    *   Any path starting X-G must use (G, B) or (G, H) to reach the C-side (other than going to F or K, which are also in the X-cluster). Removing (G, B) and (G, H) cuts these paths.
    *   Any path starting X-K must use (K, F) or (K, G). If it uses (K, F), it eventually relies on (F, E). If it uses (K, G), it eventually relies on (G, B) or (G, H). All these are covered by the cut set.

    Therefore, removing these three edges completely disconnects X from C.

7.  **Conclusion:**
    Since we found 3 edge-disjoint paths, the minimum cut is at least 3. We also found a cut set of 3 edges that successfully disconnects X from C. Thus, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

    *   If a path from J to L uses E as its penultimate node, it must use the edge (E, L).

    *   Removing (E, L) breaks all paths of the form J - ... - E - L.
    Since there are no other direct connections to L, removing these two edges guarantees disconnection.

5.  **Check for minimality:**
    If only one edge were removed (e.g., (M, L)), then paths through E (e.g., J-D-P-O-N-B-E-L) would still exist.

Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are E and H. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect E from H. This is a min-cut problem, and by Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between E and H.

First, I will identify all distinct paths between E and H and try to find the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths.

Let's list the direct neighbors of E and H:
Neighbors of E: {H, F, G, L, M}
Neighbors of H: {E, F, G, B, I}

Now, let's identify edge-disjoint paths:

1.  **Path 1 (Direct):** E - H
    Edges: `(E, H)`

2.  **Path 2 (Via F):** E - F - H
    Edges: `(E, F)`, `(F, H)`
    This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1.

3.  **Path 3 (Via G):** E - G - H
    Edges: `(E, G)`, `(G, H)`
    This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2 (as `(E,G)` is distinct from `(E,F)` and `(E,H)`, and `(G,H)` is distinct from `(F,H)` and `(E,H)`).

4.  **Path 4 (Via L, M, J, I):** E - L - M - J - I - H
    Edges: `(E, L)`, `(L, M)`, `(M, J)`, `(J, I)`, `(I, H)`
    Let's check if these edges are disjoint from Paths 1, 2, and 3:
    - `(E, L)` is distinct from `(E, H)`, `(E, F)`, `(E, G)`.
    - `(L, M)` is a new edge.
    - `(M, J)` is a new edge.
    - `(J, I)` is a new edge.
    - `(I, H)` is distinct from `(E, H)`, `(F, H)`, `(G, H)`.
    All edges in Path 4 are distinct from the edges in Paths 1, 2, and 3. Thus, this is a fourth edge-disjoint path.

Now, I will attempt to find a fifth edge-disjoint path.
To do this, I would need to use remaining edges from E to its neighbors (only `(E, M)` is left if `(E,L)` is used in P4) and remaining edges from H to its neighbors (only `(B, H)` is left if `(I,H)` is used in P4).

Let's try to construct a path using `(E, M)` and `(B, H)`:
Candidate Path 5: E - M - J - P - O - N - B - H
Edges: `(E, M)`, `(M, J)`, `(J, P)`, `(P, O)`, `(O, N)`, `(N, B)`, `(B, H)`

Let's check for edge-disjointness with the existing four paths:
- Path 1: `(E, H)` - No common edges.
- Path 2: `(E, F)`, `(F, H)` - No common edges.
- Path 3: `(E, G)`, `(G, H)` - No common edges.
- Path 4: `(E, L)`, `(L, M)`, `(M, J)`, `(J, I)`, `(I, H)`
    - The edge `(M, J)` is present in both Path 4 and the candidate Path 5.
    Therefore, this candidate path is NOT edge-disjoint from Path 4.

This indicates that `(M, J)` is a critical edge for all paths from E (via L or M) to the "right" part of the graph (J, I, P, D, C, B, N, O, Y). Specifically, any path from E that goes through L or M to reach J, I, P, D, C, B, N, O, Y must pass through `(M, J)`. Since H is connected to this "right" part via I and B, `(M, J)` acts as a bottleneck for all such paths.

Since I have found 4 edge-disjoint paths and could not find a 5th, the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths is 4. By Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect E and H is 4.

Now, I need to identify a cut set of 4 edges that achieves this disconnection. Based on the analysis of bottlenecks:
1.  The direct path `(E, H)` must be cut.
2.  All paths through the "left cluster" (nodes F, G, K, X) must be cut. These paths use edges `(E, F)` or `(E, G)` to leave E, and `(F, H)` or `(G, H)` to reach H. To cut all of them, we need to remove at least two edges (e.g., `(E, F)` and `(E, G)`).
3.  All paths through the "right cluster" (nodes L, M, J, I, P, D, C, B, N, O, Y) must be cut. As identified, the edge `(M, J)` is a critical bottleneck for all paths from E (via L or M) to the rest of this cluster, which then leads to H (via I or B). So, `(M, J)` must be cut.

Let's propose a cut set based on this:
- `(E, H)` (cuts the direct path)
- `(E, F)` (cuts paths from E to F)
- `(E, G)` (cuts paths from E to G)
- `(M, J)` (cuts all paths from E via L/M to the rest of the right cluster, and thus to I/B/H)

Let's verify this cut set: `[(E, H), (E, F), (E, G), (M, J)]`
1.  Removing `(E, H)` breaks the direct connection.
2.  Removing `(E, F)` and `(E, G)` ensures that E cannot reach nodes F or G. Since F and G are the only nodes in the left cluster connected to E, all paths from E through the left cluster to H are broken.
3.  Removing `(M, J)` ensures that E cannot reach node J. Since E can only reach J (and subsequently I or B, which connect to H) via L-M-J or M-J, cutting `(M, J)` disconnects E from the entire right cluster that leads to H.

With these 4 edges removed, no path exists between E and H. Since we established that the minimum cut is 4, this set is a minimal cut set.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 4, Cut Set: [(E, H), (E, F), (E, G), (M, J)]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 3, 'cut_set': [[['H', 'B'], ['H', 'I'], ['E', 'F']], [['E', 'B'], ['E', 'F'], ['M', 'J']]]}

Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 6
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are E and J. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to ensure no path exists between E and J.

1.  **Identify the target nodes and their direct connections:**
    *   Node E is connected to: L, M, P, B.
    *   Node J is connected to: M, D, P, I.

2.  **Analyze paths and potential cut points:**
    Any path from E to J must eventually reach one of J's direct neighbors (M, D, P, I) and then traverse an edge to J.

    *   **Consider node D:** Node D is only connected to J. There are no other edges connected to D. This means D cannot be an intermediate node in any path from E to J. Therefore, the edge (D, J) does not need to be removed to disconnect E from J, as E cannot reach D.

    *   **Remaining direct connections to J:** All paths from E to J must end by traversing one of the edges: (M, J), (P, J), or (I, J).

3.  **Formulate a potential cut set:**
    If we remove the edges (M, J), (P, J), and (I, J), then J will be disconnected from all nodes that E can reach. Let's verify this:
    *   **Path E - M - J:** This path is broken by removing (M, J).
    *   **Path E - P - J:** This path is broken by removing (P, J).
    *   **Paths through I to J (e.g., E - B - C - I - J, E - B - H - I - J, E - P - I - J):** All these paths are broken by removing (I, J).
    *   **Paths through L to M to J (e.g., E - L - M - J):** This path is broken by removing (M, J).
    *   **Paths through B to P to J (e.g., E - B - N - O - P - J):** This path is broken by removing (P, J).

    Since all paths from E to J must use one of the edges (M, J), (P, J), or (I, J) to reach J, removing these three edges will completely disconnect E from J. This gives a cut set of size 3.

4.  **Check for a smaller cut (e.g., 2 edges):**
    Let's assume we can disconnect E and J by removing only two edges.
    *   If we remove (M, J) and (P, J): Paths like E - B - C - I - J or E - B - H - I - J would still exist, as (I, J) is intact.
    *   If we remove (M, J) and (I, J): Paths like E - P - J would still exist, as (P, J) is intact.
    *   If we remove (P, J) and (I, J): Paths like E - M - J or E - L - M - J would still exist, as (M, J) is intact.

    Since removing any two of these three critical edges leaves at least one path between E and J, a cut of size 2 is not possible.

5.  **Conclusion:**
    The minimum number of edges to remove is 3, and the cut set consists of the edges directly connecting M, P, and I to J.

The final answer is:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(M, J), (P, J), (I, J)]

    *   **[B, F]:** Paths through M and L remain. For example, I-J-M-E is still a valid path.
    *   **[B, M]:** Paths through F and L remain. For example, I-H-G-F-E is still a valid path. (Note: L is connected to M and E. If M is removed, L cannot be reached from I, so paths through L are also cut).
    *   **[B, L]:** Paths through F and M remain. For example, I-H-G-F-E and I-J-M-E are still valid paths.

    *   **[F, L]:** Paths through B and M remain. For example, I-C-B-E and I-J-M-E are still valid paths.

    No pair of these "gateway" nodes disconnects I and E. This suggests the minimum cut set is larger than 2.

5.  **Identify Vertex-Disjoint Paths:** To confirm the minimum number of nodes, I will look for vertex-disjoint paths between I and E (excluding I and E themselves).

    *   **Removing F:** All paths that go through F to E are severed (e.g., I-H-G-F-E, I-H-X-K-F-E). This covers all paths originating from I-H.
    *   **Removing M:** All paths that go through M to E are severed (e.g., I-J-M-E, I-C-O-P-D-J-M-E). Additionally, node L is only connected to M and E. If M is removed, L becomes unreachable from I, effectively severing any path through L to E.

    With B, F, and M removed, all direct connections to E (B, F, M, L) are either removed or made unreachable from I. Therefore, no path remains between I and E.

7.  **Conclusion:** Since we found 3 vertex-disjoint paths, the minimum cut size is at least 3. We also found a cut set of size 3 ([B, F, M]) that successfully disconnects I and E. Thus, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

3.  **Determine if a smaller cut (e.g., 1 or 2 edges) is possible:**
    To prove that 3 is the minimum, we can try to find 3 edge-disjoint paths between E and H. If we can find 3 edge-disjoint paths, then by Menger's Theorem, at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect E and H.

    Let's identify three edge-disjoint paths from E to H:
    *   **Path 1:** E - B - H
        *   Edges: (E, B), (B, H)
    *   **Path 2:** E - F - G - H

    Let's verify that these three paths are indeed edge-disjoint (i.e., they do not share any common edges):
    *   Edges in Path 1: (E,B), (B,H)
    *   Edges in Path 2: (E,F), (F,G), (G,H)
    *   Edges in Path 3: (E,L), (L,M), (M,F), (F,K), (K,J), (J,I), (I,H)

    Since we have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between E and H, according to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect E and H is at least 3.

    We previously identified a cut set of size 3: `[(G, H), (B, H), (I, H)]`.

    *   Removing (B, H) breaks all paths from E to H that pass through B.
    *   Removing (I, H) breaks all paths from E to H that pass through I.
    Since any path from E to H must pass through one of H's direct neighbors (G, B, or I), removing these three edges guarantees that no path exists between E and H.

    Since we found a cut set of size 3, and we proved that the minimum cut size must be at least 3, the minimum number of edges to remove is exactly 3.

Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 6
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are H and N. The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect H and N.

1.  **Identify all possible paths between H and N.**
    Let's list the direct neighbors of H: B, I, E, G.
    Let's list the direct neighbors of N: B, C, O, Y.
    Node Y is only connected to N and O. Any path from H to N through Y would have to be H-...-O-Y-N, but (O,Y) is not an edge, and Y is not connected to H directly or indirectly without passing through N. So, Y is a dead-end for paths from H to N. Therefore, all paths from H to N must enter N via B, C, or O.

    Let's categorize the paths:
    *   **Paths through B:**
        *   H - B - N
    *   **Paths through I:**
        *   H - I - C - N
        *   H - I - P - O - N
    *   **Paths through E:**
        *   H - E - M - J - P - O - N
        *   H - E - L - M - J - P - O - N
    *   **Paths through G:**
        *   H - G - F - E - M - J - P - O - N
        *   H - G - F - E - L - M - J - P - O - N

2.  **Determine the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths.**
    To find the minimum number of edges to remove (min-cut), we can use Menger's Theorem, which states that the minimum number of edges in an edge cut is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between the two nodes.

    Let's identify three edge-disjoint paths:
    *   **Path 1:** H - B - N
        Edges: (H, B), (B, N)
    *   **Path 2:** H - I - C - N
        Edges: (H, I), (I, C), (C, N)
    *   **Path 3:** H - E - M - J - P - O - N
        Edges: (H, E), (E, M), (M, J), (J, P), (P, O), (O, N)

    Let's verify that these three paths are edge-disjoint:
    *   The first edges from H are (H, B), (H, I), and (H, E), which are distinct.
    *   The last edges into N are (B, N), (C, N), and (O, N), which are distinct.
    *   There are no common intermediate edges between these three paths.
    Since we have found 3 edge-disjoint paths, the minimum number of edges to remove must be at least 3.

3.  **Find a cut set of 3 edges and verify its effectiveness.**
    Based on the observation that all paths must enter N via B, C, or O, consider removing the edges that connect these nodes to N:
    *   (B, N)
    *   (C, N)
    *   (O, N)

    Let's verify if removing these three edges disconnects H from N:
    *   Removing (B, N) breaks the path H - B - N.
    *   Removing (C, N) breaks the path H - I - C - N.
    *   Removing (O, N) breaks all paths that go through O to reach N:
        *   H - I - P - O - N
        *   H - E - M - J - P - O - N
        *   H - E - L - M - J - P - O - N
        *   H - G - F - E - M - J - P - O - N
        *   H - G - F - E - L - M - J - P - O - N

    After removing these three edges, N is only connected to Y. Since Y is not connected to H (except through N itself), N is completely disconnected from H.

Since we have found a cut set of 3 edges that disconnects H and N, and we previously established that at least 3 edges are required (due to 3 edge-disjoint paths), the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(B, N), (C, N), (O, N)]

2.  **Identify Neighbors:**
    *   Neighbors of E: M, L, B
    *   Neighbors of I: P, C, H

3.  **Find Node-Disjoint Paths:** To determine the minimum number of nodes to remove, I will look for node-disjoint paths between E and I. Node-disjoint paths are paths that do not share any intermediate nodes (nodes other than E and I themselves).

    I can identify the following three node-disjoint paths:
    *   **Path 1:** E → M → J → P → I
        *   Intermediate nodes: {M, J, P}
    *   **Path 2:** E → B → C → I
        *   Intermediate nodes: {B, C}
    *   **Path 3:** E → L → F → K → G → H → I
        *   Intermediate nodes: {L, F, K, G, H}

4.  **Verify Node-Disjointness:**
    *   The set of intermediate nodes for Path 1 is {M, J, P}.
    *   The set of intermediate nodes for Path 2 is {B, C}.
    *   The set of intermediate nodes for Path 3 is {L, F, K, G, H}.
    These three sets of intermediate nodes are mutually exclusive (they do not share any common nodes). Therefore, these three paths are indeed node-disjoint.

5.  **Apply Menger's Theorem:** According to Menger's Theorem (node version), the minimum number of nodes that must be removed to disconnect two non-adjacent nodes (E and I) is equal to the maximum number of node-disjoint paths between them. Since I found 3 node-disjoint paths, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

6.  **Identify a Cut Set:** To disconnect E from I, I need to remove at least one node from each of these three node-disjoint paths. A simple way to achieve this is to remove all direct neighbors of E (or I).
    *   If I remove nodes {M, B, L}, E will have no outgoing connections to the rest of the graph.
        *   Removing M breaks Path 1 (E → M → ...).
        *   Removing B breaks Path 2 (E → B → ...).
        *   Removing L breaks Path 3 (E → L → ...).
    Since E is only connected to M, B, and L, removing these three nodes will completely disconnect E from the rest of the graph, and thus from I.

7.  **Conclusion:** The minimum number of nodes to remove is 3. The cut set {M, B, L} achieves this disconnection. Other valid cut sets of size 3 also exist, such as {P, C, H} (the direct neighbors of I) or {J, C, H}.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [M, B, L]

Image name: PG_task2_gt3_PAIR4
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 6
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The goal is to find the minimum number of nodes to remove to disconnect the two black nodes, H and E, without removing H or E themselves. This is a minimum vertex cut problem.

First, I identify the two target nodes: H and E.

Next, I systematically explore all possible paths from H to E. I will categorize these paths based on the immediate neighbors of H and E.
Neighbors of H: I, C, X, G
Neighbors of E: B, F, L

Let's identify distinct "channels" or groups of paths:

**Channel 1: Paths from H to E via F (neighbor of E)**
These paths originate from H and pass through G or X to reach F, and then E.
*   H - G - F - E
*   H - G - K - F - E
*   H - X - G - F - E
*   H - X - K - F - E
All these paths converge on node F before reaching E. Therefore, removing node F will disconnect all paths in this channel.

**Channel 2: Paths from H to E via B (neighbor of E)**
These paths originate from H and pass through I or C, then through a series of intermediate nodes, to reach B, and then E.
Let's list some representative paths:
*   H - C - N - B - E
*   H - C - O - N - B - E
*   H - I - C - N - B - E
*   H - I - C - O - N - B - E
*   H - I - P - O - N - B - E
*   H - I - J - D - P - O - N - B - E
Upon inspection, all paths from H that lead to B must pass through node N. Node N is directly connected to B, and all other paths to B (from C, O, Y) also involve N. Therefore, removing node N will disconnect all paths in this channel.

**Channel 3: Paths from H to E via L (neighbor of E)**
There is only one path from H that leads to L, and then E:
*   H - I - J - M - L - E
This path passes through node M. Therefore, removing node M will disconnect this specific path.

Based on this analysis, we have identified three critical nodes: F, N, and M.
*   Removing F disconnects all paths from H to E that go through the lower part of the graph (via G, X, K).
*   Removing N disconnects all paths from H to E that go through the upper-middle part of the graph (via I, C, O, P, D, J to B).
*   Removing M disconnects the specific path H-I-J-M-L-E, which is the only path to E via L.

If we remove the set of nodes {F, N, M}, all identified paths from H to E are broken.

To confirm that this is the *minimum* number of nodes, I will attempt to find node-disjoint paths between H and E. According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes in a vertex cut is equal to the maximum number of node-disjoint paths between the two nodes.

Let's find node-disjoint paths (excluding H and E):
1.  **Path 1:** H - G - F - E (Intermediate nodes: G, F)
2.  **Path 2:** H - I - J - M - L - E (Intermediate nodes: I, J, M, L)
3.  **Path 3:** H - C - N - B - E (Intermediate nodes: C, N, B)

Let's check if these paths are truly node-disjoint in their intermediate nodes:
*   Path 1 nodes: {G, F}
*   Path 2 nodes: {I, J, M, L}
*   Path 3 nodes: {C, N, B}
These three sets of intermediate nodes are mutually exclusive. Therefore, we have found 3 node-disjoint paths between H and E. This implies that at least 3 nodes must be removed to disconnect H and E.

Since we found a cut set of size 3 ({F, N, M}) and confirmed that at least 3 nodes are required, this is the minimum number.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [F, N, M]
